# Supernova

For other uses, see [Supernova (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_\(disambiguation\)).



[SN 1994D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1994D) (bright spot on the lower left), a [Type Ia supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_Ia_supernova) within its host galaxy, [NGC 4526](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_4526)

A **supernova** (pl.: **supernovae**) is a powerful and luminous [explosion](18993857.md) of a [star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star). A supernova occurs during the last [evolutionary stages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_evolution) of a [massive star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_star), or when a [white dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_dwarf) is triggered into runaway [nuclear fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fusion). The original object, called the *progenitor*, either collapses to a [neutron star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star) or [black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole), or is completely destroyed to form a [diffuse nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffuse_nebula). The peak optical [luminosity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminosity) of a supernova can be comparable to that of an entire [galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy) before fading over several weeks or months.

It is expected that supernovae in our galaxy occur on average once every 61 years, although the last to be observed was [Kepler's Supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler's_Supernova) in 1604. [SN 1987A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1987A) occurred in the [Large Magellanic Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Magellanic_Cloud), a [satellite galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_galaxy) of our galaxy, in 1987. Several thousand supernovae are typically seen in distant galaxies every year.

Theoretical studies indicate that most supernovae are triggered by one of two basic mechanisms: the sudden re-ignition of nuclear fusion in a [white dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_dwarf), or the sudden [gravitational collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_collapse) of a massive star's [core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_core).

- In the re-ignition of a white dwarf, the object's temperature is raised enough to trigger [runaway](2350918.md) nuclear fusion, completely disrupting the star. Possible causes are an accumulation of material from a [binary companion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_star) through [accretion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_\(astrophysics\)), or by a [stellar merger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_merger).

- In the case of a massive star's sudden implosion, the core of a [massive star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_evolution#Massive_stars) will undergo sudden collapse once it is unable to produce sufficient energy from fusion to counteract the star's own gravity, which must happen once the star begins [fusing iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_process), but may happen during an earlier stage of [metal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallicity) fusion.

Supernovae can expel several [solar masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass) of material at speeds up to several percent of the [speed of light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_of_light). This drives an expanding [shock wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_wave) into the surrounding [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium), sweeping up an expanding shell of gas and dust observed as a supernova remnant. Supernovae are a major source of [elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_element) in the interstellar medium from [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen) to [rubidium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubidium). The expanding shock waves of supernovae can trigger the [formation of new stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_formation). Supernovae are a major source of [cosmic rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_ray). They might also produce [gravitational waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_wave).

## Occurrence

The first supernovae to be studied by astronomical methods were [Tycho's Supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho's_Supernova) in 1572 and [Kepler's Supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler's_Supernova) in 1604, both of which were in the [Milky Way](2589714.md) and were visible to the [naked eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naked_eye). Analysis of the historical record suggests that, aside from telescope discoveries, fewer than 10 supernovae have been observed over the last 2,000 years.[^cite_note-1]<sup>: 6 </sup>

Observations of recent [supernova remnants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_remnant) within the Milky Way, coupled with studies of supernovae in other galaxies, suggest that these powerful stellar explosions occur in our galaxy approximately 1.6 to 4.6 times per century on average.[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-reynolds-3]

In 1987, the supernova [SN 1987A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1987A) appeared in the [Large Magellanic Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Magellanic_Cloud), a [satellite galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_galaxy) of the Milky Way in an easily studied part of the sky. Many astronomical observations were made on SN 1987A, including the only measurements of astronomical [neutrinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino) other than the Sun's. The event was attributed to an explosion of a [blue supergiant star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_supergiant_star).[^cite_note-4]

## Etymology

The word *supernova* has the [plural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plural) form *supernovae* ([/-viː/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English)) or *supernovas* and is often abbreviated as SN or SNe. It is derived from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language) word *[nova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova)*, meaning 'new', which refers to what appears to be a temporary new bright star. Adding the prefix "super-" distinguishes supernovae from ordinary novae, which are far less luminous. The word *supernova* was coined by [Walter Baade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Baade) and [Fritz Zwicky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Zwicky), who began using it in astrophysics lectures in 1931.[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-\:1-6] Its first use in a journal article came the following year in a publication by [Knut Lundmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knut_Lundmark), who may have coined it independently.[^cite_note-\:1-6][^cite_note-7]

## Observation history

Main article: [History of supernova observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_supernova_observation)

Compared to a star's entire history, the visual appearance of a supernova is very brief, sometimes spanning several months, so that the chances of observing one with the naked eye are roughly once in a lifetime. Only a tiny fraction of the 100 [billion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billion) stars in a typical [galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy) have the capacity to become a supernova, the ability being restricted to those having high mass and those in rare kinds of [binary star systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_star_system) with at least one [white dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_dwarf).[^cite_note-8]

### Early observations

A rock carving in the [Burzahama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burzahom_archaeological_site) region of [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), dated to 4500±1000 [BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Before_Christ) showing what might be nova HB9[^cite_note-Joglekar-9] is the earliest of many claimed but unverifiable records of supernovae by prehistoric people.[^cite_note-10] The first widely recorded supernova was [SN 1006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1006), observed in AD 1006 in the constellation of [Lupus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lupus_\(constellation\)).[^cite_note-Stephenson-1976-11] This event was described by observers in China, Japan, Iraq, Egypt and Europe.[^cite_note-Murdin-12][^cite_note-13][^cite_note-winkler-14]

The supernova [SN 1054](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1054), which produced the [Crab Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crab_Nebula), was recorded by Chinese astronomers in AD 1054.[^cite_note-\:0-15] Supernovae [SN 1572](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1572) and [SN 1604](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1604), the latest Milky Way supernovae to be observed with the naked eye, had a notable influence on the development of astronomy in [Europe](9239.md) because they were used to argue against the [Aristotelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) idea that the universe beyond the Moon and planets was static and unchanging.[^cite_note-16] [Johannes Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler) began observing SN 1604 at its peak on 17 October 1604, and continued to make estimates of its brightness until it faded from naked eye view a year later.[^cite_note-kepler-17] It was the second supernova to be observed in a generation, after [Tycho Brahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho_Brahe) observed SN 1572 in [Cassiopeia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassiopeia_A).[^cite_note-history-18]

There is some evidence that the youngest known supernova in our galaxy, [G1.9+0.3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G1.9+0.3), occurred in the late 19th century, considerably more recently than [Cassiopeia A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassiopeia_A) from around 1680.[^cite_note-chakraborti-19] Neither was noted at the time. In the case of G1.9+0.3, high [extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction_\(astronomy\)) from dust along the plane of the galactic disk could have dimmed the event sufficiently for it to go unnoticed. The situation for Cassiopeia A is less clear; infrared [light echoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_echo) have been detected showing that it was not in a region of especially high extinction.[^cite_note-krause-20]



The [Crab Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crab_Nebula) is a [pulsar wind nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulsar_wind_nebula) associated with the [1054 supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1054_supernova).



A 1414 text cites a 1055 report: since "the baleful star appeared, a full year has passed and until now its brilliance has not faded".

### Telescope findings

With the development of the astronomical [telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telescope), observation and discovery of fainter and more distant supernovae became possible. The first such observation was of [SN 1885A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1885A) in the [Andromeda Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy). A decade later, two further supernovae, [SN 1895A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1895A?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [SN 1895B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1895B), were discovered in [NGC 4424](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_4424) and [NGC 5253](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_5253) respectively.[^cite_note-Schaefer1995-29][^cite_note-30] Early work on what was originally believed to be simply a new category of [novae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova) was performed during the 1920s. These were variously called "upper-class Novae", "Hauptnovae", or "giant novae".[^cite_note-31] The name "supernovae" is thought to have been coined by [Walter Baade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Baade) and [Fritz Zwicky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Zwicky) in lectures at [Caltech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caltech) in 1931. It was used, as "super-Novae", in a journal paper published by [Knut Lundmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knut_Lundmark) in 1933,[^cite_note-baas33_1330-32] and in a 1934 paper by Baade and Zwicky.[^cite_note-Baade1934-33] By 1938, the hyphen was no longer used and the modern name was in use.[^cite_note-34]

[Rudolph Minkowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolph_Minkowski) and Fritz Zwicky developed the modern supernova classification scheme beginning in 1941.[^cite_note-35] During the 1960s, astronomers found that the maximum intensities of supernovae could be used as [standard candles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_candles), hence indicators of astronomical distances.[^cite_note-36] Some of the most distant supernovae observed in 2003 appeared dimmer than expected. This supports the view that the expansion of the [universe is accelerating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accelerating_universe).[^cite_note-Leibundgut-37] Techniques were developed for reconstructing supernovae events that have no written records of being observed. The date of the Cassiopeia A supernova event was determined from light echoes off [nebulae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebula),[^cite_note-38] while the age of supernova remnant [RX J0852.0-4622](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RX_J0852.0-4622) was estimated from temperature measurements[^cite_note-39] and the [gamma ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_ray) emissions from the radioactive decay of <sup>44</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ti.[^cite_note-40]



Jades Deep Field. A team of astronomers studying JADES data identified about 80 objects (circled in green) that changed in brightness over time. Most of these objects, known as transients, are the result of exploding stars or supernovae.

[^cite_note-41]

The most luminous supernova ever recorded is [ASASSN-15lh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASASSN-15lh), at a distance of 3.82 [gigalight-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year). It was first detected in June 2015 and peaked at 570 billion [L<sub>☉</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_luminosity), which is twice the [bolometric luminosity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolometric_luminosity) of any other known supernova.[^cite_note-42] The nature of this supernova is debated and several alternative explanations, such as tidal disruption of a star by a black hole, have been suggested.[^cite_note-43]

[SN 2013fs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2013fs) was recorded three hours after the supernova event on 6 October 2013, by the [Intermediate Palomar Transient Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palomar_Transient_Factory). This is among the earliest supernovae caught after detonation, and it is the earliest for which spectra have been obtained, beginning six hours after the actual explosion. The star is located in a [spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiral_galaxy) named [NGC 7610](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_7610), 160 million light-years away in the constellation of Pegasus.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45]

The supernova [SN 2016gkg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2016gkg) was detected by amateur astronomer Victor Buso from [Rosario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosario), Argentina, on 20 September 2016.[^cite_note-AS_Now-46][^cite_note-NatBuso-47] It was the first time that the initial "shock breakout" from an optical supernova had been observed.[^cite_note-AS_Now-46] The progenitor star has been identified in [Hubble Space Telescope](40203.md) images from before its collapse. Astronomer [Alex Filippenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Filippenko) noted: "Observations of stars in the first moments they begin exploding provide information that cannot be directly obtained in any other way."[^cite_note-AS_Now-46]

### Discovery programs



[Supernova remnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_remnant) SNR E0519-69.0 in the [Large Magellanic Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Magellanic_Cloud)

Because supernovae are relatively rare events within a galaxy, occurring about three times a century in the Milky Way,[^cite_note-reynolds-3] obtaining a good sample of supernovae to study requires regular monitoring of many galaxies. Today, amateur and professional astronomers are finding about two thousand every year, some when near maximum brightness, others on old astronomical photographs or plates. Supernovae in other galaxies cannot be predicted with any meaningful accuracy. Normally, when they are discovered, they are already in progress.[^cite_note-48] To use supernovae as [standard candles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_candle) for measuring distance, observation of their peak luminosity is required. It is therefore important to discover them well before they reach their maximum. [Amateur astronomers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amateur_astronomy), who greatly outnumber professional astronomers, have played an important role in finding supernovae, typically by looking at some of the closer galaxies through an [optical telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_telescope) and comparing them to earlier photographs.[^cite_note-49]

Toward the end of the 20th century, astronomers increasingly turned to computer-controlled telescopes and [CCDs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charge-coupled_device) for hunting supernovae. While such systems are popular with amateurs, there are also professional installations such as the [Katzman Automatic Imaging Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katzman_Automatic_Imaging_Telescope).[^cite_note-50] The [Supernova Early Warning System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_Early_Warning_System) (SNEWS) project uses a network of [neutrino detectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino_detector) to give early warning of a supernova in the Milky Way galaxy.[^cite_note-Antonioli-2004-51][^cite_note-52] [Neutrinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino) are [subatomic particles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subatomic_particle) that are produced in great quantities by a supernova, and they are not significantly absorbed by the interstellar gas and dust of the galactic disk.[^cite_note-53]



"A star set to explode", the SBW1 nebula surrounds a massive blue supergiant in the [Carina Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carina_Nebula).

Supernova searches fall into two classes: those focused on relatively nearby events and those looking farther away. Because of the [expansion of the universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_the_universe), the distance to a remote object with a known [emission spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_spectrum) can be estimated by measuring its [Doppler shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppler_shift) (or [redshift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redshift)); on average, more-distant objects recede with greater velocity than those nearby, and so have a higher redshift. Thus the search is split between high redshift and low redshift, with the boundary falling around a redshift range of z=0.1–0.3, where z is a dimensionless measure of the spectrum's frequency shift.[^cite_note-54]

High-redshift searches for supernovae usually involve the observation of supernova light curves. These are useful for standard or calibrated candles to generate [Hubble diagrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubble_diagram) and make cosmological predictions. Supernova spectroscopy, used to study the physics and environments of supernovae, is more practical at low than at high redshift.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] Low redshift observations also anchor the low-distance end of the [Hubble curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubble_curve), which is a plot of distance versus redshift for visible galaxies.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

As survey programmes rapidly increase the number of detected supernovae, collated collections of observations (light decay curves, astrometry, pre-supernova observations, spectroscopy) have been assembled. The Pantheon data set, assembled in 2018, detailed 1048 supernovae.[^cite_note-59] In 2021, this data set was expanded to 1701 light curves for 1550 supernovae taken from 18 different surveys, a 50% increase in under 3 years.[^cite_note-60]

## Naming convention



Multi-wavelength [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray), [infrared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared), and [optical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical) compilation image of [Kepler's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler) [supernova remnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_remnant), [SN 1604](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1604)

Supernova discoveries are reported to the [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union)'s [Central Bureau for Astronomical Telegrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bureau_for_Astronomical_Telegrams), which sends out a circular with the name it assigns to that supernova.[^cite_note-61] The name is formed from the prefix *SN*, followed by the year of discovery, suffixed with a one- to four-letter designation. The first 26 supernovae of the year are designated with a capital letter from *A* to *Z*. Next, pairs of lower-case letters are used: *aa*, *ab*, and so on.[^cite_note-\:3-62] After zz comes aaa, then aab, aac, and so on.

Hence, for example, SN 2003C designates the third supernova reported in the year 2003.[^cite_note-\:3-62] The last reported supernova of 2005, SN 2005nc, was the 367th (14 × 26 + 3 = 367). The last supernova retained in the Asiago Supernova Catalogue when it was terminated on 31 December 2017 bears the designation SN 2017jzp.[^cite_note-asiago-63] [SN 2021afdx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2021afdx) is an example of a four-letter suffix.

Historical supernovae are known simply by the year they occurred: SN 185, SN 1006, SN 1054, SN 1572 (called *Tycho's Nova*) and SN 1604 (*Kepler's Star*).[^cite_note-64] Since 1885 the additional letter notation has been used, even if there was only one supernova discovered that year (for example, SN 1885A, SN 1907A, etc.); this last happened with SN 1947A.[^cite_note-\:4-65]

Until 1987, two-letter designations were rarely needed; since 1988, they have been needed every year.[^cite_note-\:4-65] Since 2000, professional and amateur astronomers have been finding several hundred supernovae each year (572 in 2007, 261 in 2008, 390 in 2009; 231 in 2013).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-asiago-63] Since 2016, the increasing number of discoveries has regularly led to the additional use of three-letter designations.[^cite_note-\:4-65]

## Classification

Astronomers classify supernovae according to their [light curves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_curve) and the [absorption lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absorption_line) of different [chemical elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_element) that appear in their [spectra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_spectroscopy). If a supernova's spectrum contains lines of [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen) (known as the [Balmer series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balmer_series) in the visual portion of the spectrum) it is classified *Type II*; otherwise it is *Type I*. In each of these two types there are subdivisions according to the presence of lines from other elements or the shape of the light curve (a graph of the supernova's apparent magnitude as a function of time).[^cite_note-types-67][^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

| Type I No hydrogen | [Type Ia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_Ia_supernova) Presents a singly [ionised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionization) [silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon) (Si II) line at 615.0 [nm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanometre) (nanometers), near peak light | [Thermal runaway](#Thermal_runaway) |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Type Ib/c](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_Ib_and_Ic_supernovae) Weak or no silicon absorption feature | Type Ib Shows a non-ionised [helium](13256.md) (He I) line at 587.6 nm | [Core collapse](#Core_collapse) |  |
| Type Ic Weak or no helium |  |  |  |
| [Type II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova) Shows hydrogen | [Type II-P/-L/n](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova) Type II spectrum throughout | [Type II-P/L](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova#Light_curves_for_Type_II-L_and_Type_II-P_supernovae) No narrow lines | [Type II-P](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova#Light_curves_for_Type_II-L_and_Type_II-P_supernovae) Reaches a "plateau" in its light curve |
| [Type II-L](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova#Light_curves_for_Type_II-L_and_Type_II-P_supernovae) Displays a "linear" decrease in its light curve (linear in magnitude versus time)[^cite_note-doggett-69] |  |  |  |
| [Type IIn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova#Type_IIn_supernovae) Some narrow lines |  |  |  |
| [Type IIb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova#Type_IIb_supernovae) Spectrum changes to become like Type Ib |  |  |  |

### Type I



Light curve for Type Ia [SN 2018gv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_2525#SN_2018gv)

Type I supernovae are subdivided on the basis of their spectra, with Type Ia showing a strong [ionised silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon) absorption line. Type I supernovae without this strong line are classified as Type Ib and Ic, with Type Ib showing strong neutral helium lines and Type Ic lacking them. Historically, the light curves of Type I supernovae were seen as all broadly similar, too much so to make useful distinctions.[^cite_note-doggett-69] While variations in light curves have been studied, classification continues to be made on spectral grounds rather than light-curve shape.[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

A small number of Type Ia supernovae exhibit unusual features, such as non-standard luminosity or broadened light curves, and these are typically categorised by referring to the earliest example showing similar features. For example, the sub-luminous [SN 2008ha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2008ha) is often referred to as [SN 2002cx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2002cx)-like or class Ia-2002cx.[^cite_note-foley2008-70]

A small proportion of Type Ic supernovae show highly broadened and blended emission lines which are taken to indicate very high expansion velocities for the ejecta. These have been classified as Type Ic-BL or Ic-bl.[^cite_note-bianco-71]

[Calcium-rich supernovae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium-rich_supernova) are a rare type of very fast supernova with unusually strong calcium lines in their spectra.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] Models suggest they occur when material is accreted from a [helium](13256.md)-rich companion rather than a [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen)-rich star. Because of helium lines in their spectra, they can resemble Type Ib supernovae, but are thought to have very different progenitors.[^cite_note-Perets-2010-74]

Type Ien has been proposed to explain observations of the supernova [SN 2021yfj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2021yfj).[^cite_note-schulze-75] Having lost its outer layers of hydrogen, helium and carbon, the star, just before the explosion, released an unusual, hidden layer of [silicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon), [sulfur](27127.md) and [argon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argon), elements that are not often seen in dying stars.[^cite_note-cnn-76] During the explosion, the material from the star's [core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_core) collided with the gaseous shell, and the heat of the collision caused the silicon and sulfur layer to glow. The explosion showed that stars can be completely stripped down and still produce a brilliant explosion observable from very far distance. The discovery provided direct evidence of the long-theorized, but difficult to observe, [internal structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_structure) of [massive stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star#Massive_stars).[^cite_note-cnn-76] In the type's name, "e" describes the position of the silicon/sulfur layer in the internal structure, while "n" signifies narrow emission lines.[^cite_note-schulze-75]

### Type II

Main article: [Type II supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova)



Light curves are used to classify Type II-P and Type II-L supernovae.

[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

[^cite_note-barbon1979-77]

The supernovae of [Type II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova) can also be sub-divided based on their spectra. While most Type II supernovae show very broad [emission lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emission_line) which indicate expansion velocities of many thousands of [kilometres per second](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilometres_per_second), some, such as [SN 2005gl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2005gl), have relatively narrow features in their spectra. These are called Type IIn, where the "n" stands for "narrow".[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

A few supernovae, such as SN 1987K[^cite_note-78] and [SN 1993J](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1993J), appear to change types: they show lines of hydrogen at early times, but, over a period of weeks to months, become dominated by lines of helium. The term ["Type IIb"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_IIb_supernova) is used to describe the combination of features normally associated with Type II and Type Ib.[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

Type II supernovae with normal spectra dominated by broad hydrogen lines that remain for the life of the decline are classified on the basis of their light curves. The most common type shows a distinctive "plateau" in the light curve shortly after peak brightness where the visual luminosity stays relatively constant for several months before the decline resumes. These are called Type II-P referring to the plateau. Less common are Type II-L supernovae that lack a distinct plateau. The "L" signifies "linear" although the light curve is not actually a straight line.[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

Supernovae that do not fit into the normal classifications are designated peculiar, or "pec".[^cite_note-taxonomy-68]

### Types III, IV and V

Zwicky defined additional supernovae types based on a very few examples that did not cleanly fit the parameters for Type I or Type II supernovae. [SN 1961i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1961i) in [NGC 4303](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_4303) was the prototype and only member of the Type III supernova class, noted for its broad light curve maximum and broad hydrogen Balmer lines that were slow to develop in the spectrum.[^cite_note-doggett-69] SN 1961f in [NGC 3003](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_3003) was the prototype and only member of the Type IV class, with a light curve similar to a Type II-P supernova, with [hydrogen absorption lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_absorption_line) but weak [hydrogen emission lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_emission_line).[^cite_note-doggett-69] The Type V class was coined for [SN 1961V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1961V) in [NGC 1058](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_1058), an unusual faint supernova or [supernova impostor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_impostor) with a slow rise to brightness, a maximum lasting many months, and an unusual emission spectrum. The similarity of SN 1961V to the [Eta Carinae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eta_Carinae) Great Outburst was noted.[^cite_note-zwicky2-79] Supernovae in M101 (1909) and M83 (1923 and 1957) were also suggested as possible Type IV or Type V supernovae.[^cite_note-zwicky-80]

These types would now all be treated as peculiar Type II supernovae (IIpec), of which many more examples have been discovered, although it is still debated whether SN 1961V was a true supernova following an [LBV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminous_blue_variable) outburst or an impostor.[^cite_note-doggett-69][^cite_note-81]

## Current models



In the galaxy [NGC 1365](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_1365) a supernova (the bright dot slightly above the galactic center) rapidly brightens, then fades more slowly.

[^cite_note-82]

Supernova type codes, as summarised in the table above, are *[taxonomic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(general\))*: the type number is based on the light observed from the supernova, not necessarily its cause. For example, Type Ia supernovae are produced by runaway fusion ignited on degenerate white dwarf progenitors, while the spectrally similar Type Ib/c are produced from massive stripped progenitor stars by core collapse.

### Thermal runaway

Main article: [Type Ia supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_Ia_supernova)



Formation of a Type Ia supernova

A white dwarf star may accumulate sufficient material from a stellar companion to raise its core temperature enough to [ignite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_detonation) [carbon fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_burning_process), at which point it undergoes [runaway](2350918.md) nuclear fusion, completely disrupting it. There are three avenues by which this detonation is theorised to happen: stable [accretion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_\(astrophysics\)) of material from a companion, the collision of two white dwarfs, or accretion that causes ignition in a shell that then ignites the core. The dominant mechanism by which Type Ia supernovae are produced remains unclear.[^cite_note-Piro2014-83] Despite this uncertainty in how Type Ia supernovae are produced, Type Ia supernovae have very uniform properties and are useful [standard candles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_distance_ladder) over intergalactic distances. Some calibrations are required to compensate for the gradual change in properties or different frequencies of abnormal luminosity supernovae at high redshift, and for small variations in brightness identified by light curve shape or spectrum.[^cite_note-chen-84][^cite_note-85]

#### Normal Type Ia

There are several means by which a supernova of this type can form, but they share a common underlying mechanism. If a [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon)-[oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen) white dwarf accreted enough matter to reach the [Chandrasekhar limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrasekhar_limit) of about 1.44 [solar masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass)[^cite_note-Mazzali2007-86] (for a non-rotating star), then it would no longer be able to support the bulk of its mass through [electron degeneracy pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_degeneracy_pressure)[^cite_note-Chandrasekhar-87][^cite_note-canal1997-88] and would begin to collapse. However, the current view is that this limit is not normally attained; increasing temperature and density inside the core ignite carbon fusion as the star approaches the limit (to within about 1%)[^cite_note-89] before collapse is initiated.[^cite_note-Mazzali2007-86] In contrast, for a core primarily composed of oxygen, neon and magnesium, the collapsing white dwarf will typically form a [neutron star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star). In this case, only a fraction of the star's mass will be ejected during the collapse.[^cite_note-canal1997-88]



The blue spot at the centre of the red ring is an isolated neutron star in the [Small Magellanic Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Magellanic_Cloud).

Within a few seconds of the collapse process, a substantial fraction of the matter in the white dwarf undergoes nuclear fusion, releasing enough energy (1–2×10<sup>44</sup> [J](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joule))[^cite_note-aaa270-90] to [unbind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_binding_energy) the star in a supernova.[^cite_note-ropke-91] An outwardly expanding [shock wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_wave) is generated, with matter reaching velocities on the order of 5,000–20,000 [km/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilometers_per_second), or roughly 3% of the speed of light. There is also a significant increase in luminosity, reaching an [absolute magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_magnitude) of −19.3 (or 5 billion times brighter than the Sun), with little variation.[^cite_note-explosion_model-92]

The model for the formation of this category of supernova is a close binary star system. The more massive of the two stars is the first to [evolve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_evolution) off the [main sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_sequence), and it expands to form a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant). The two stars now share a common envelope, causing their mutual orbit to shrink. The giant star then sheds most of its envelope, losing mass until it can no longer continue [nuclear fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fusion). At this point, it becomes a white dwarf star, composed primarily of carbon and oxygen.[^cite_note-93] Eventually, the secondary star also evolves off the main sequence to form a red giant. Matter from the giant is accreted by the white dwarf, causing the latter to increase in mass. The exact details of initiation and of the heavy elements produced in the catastrophic event remain unclear.[^cite_note-94]

Type Ia supernovae produce a characteristic light curve—the graph of luminosity as a function of time—after the event. This luminosity is generated by the [radioactive decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_decay) of [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni-56) through [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-56) to [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Fe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fe-56).[^cite_note-explosion_model-92] The peak luminosity of the light curve is extremely consistent across normal Type Ia supernovae, having a maximum absolute magnitude of about −19.3. This is because typical Type Ia supernovae arise from a consistent type of progenitor star by gradual mass acquisition, and explode when they acquire a consistent typical mass, giving rise to very similar supernova conditions and behaviour. This allows them to be used as a secondary[^cite_note-95] standard candle to measure the distance to their host galaxies.[^cite_note-96]

A second model for the formation of Type Ia supernovae involves the merger of two white dwarf stars, with the combined mass momentarily exceeding the Chandrasekhar limit.[^cite_note-97] This is sometimes referred to as the double-degenerate model, as both stars are degenerate white dwarfs. Due to the possible combinations of mass and chemical composition of the pair there is much variation in this type of event,[^cite_note-98] and, in many cases, there may be no supernova at all, in which case they will have a less luminous light curve than the more normal SN Type Ia.[^cite_note-99]

#### Non-standard Type Ia

Abnormally bright Type Ia supernovae occur when the white dwarf already has a mass higher than the Chandrasekhar limit,[^cite_note-100] possibly enhanced further by asymmetry,[^cite_note-101] but the ejected material will have less than normal kinetic energy. This super-Chandrasekhar-mass scenario can occur, for example, when the extra mass is supported by [differential rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Differential_rotation).[^cite_note-102]

There is no formal sub-classification for non-standard Type Ia supernovae. It has been proposed that a group of sub-luminous supernovae that occur when helium accretes onto a white dwarf should be classified as **Type Iax**.[^cite_note-wang-103][^cite_note-foley-104] This type of supernova may not always completely destroy the white dwarf progenitor and could leave behind a [zombie star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zombie_star).[^cite_note-mccully-105]

One specific type of supernova originates from exploding white dwarfs, like Type Ia, but contains hydrogen lines in their spectra, possibly because the white dwarf is surrounded by an envelope of hydrogen-rich [circumstellar material](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumstellar_disc). These supernovae have been dubbed Type Ia/IIn, Type Ian, Type IIa and Type IIan,[^cite_note-106] with the **type Ia-CSM** label being more widely used for these objects in the modern literature.[^cite_note-107]

The quadruple star [HD 74438](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HD_74438), belonging to the open cluster [IC 2391](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IC_2391) the [Vela constellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vela_constellation), has been predicted to become a non-standard Type Ia supernova.[^cite_note-GIAISURVEY-108][^cite_note-supernova-109]

### Core collapse



The layers of a massive, evolved star just before core collapse (not to scale).

Very massive stars can undergo core collapse when nuclear fusion becomes unable to sustain the core against its own gravity; passing this threshold is the cause of all types of supernova except Type Ia. The collapse may cause violent expulsion of the outer layers of the star resulting in a supernova. However, if the release of gravitational potential energy is insufficient, the star may instead collapse into a [black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole) or [neutron star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star) with little radiated energy.[^cite_note-heger-110]

Core collapse can be caused by several different mechanisms: exceeding the [Chandrasekhar limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandrasekhar_limit); [electron capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_capture); [pair-instability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pair-instability_supernova); or [photodisintegration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photodisintegration).[^cite_note-heger-110][^cite_note-renzon2018-111][^cite_note-nomoto-112]

- When a massive star develops an iron core larger than the Chandrasekhar mass it will no longer be able to support itself by [electron degeneracy pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_degeneracy_pressure) and will collapse further to a neutron star or black hole.

- Electron capture by magnesium in a [degenerate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degenerate_matter) O/Ne/Mg core (about 9–10 solar mass progenitor star under solar Z) removes support and causes [gravitational collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_collapse) followed by explosive oxygen fusion, with very similar results.

- Electron-positron [pair production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pair_production) in a large post-helium burning core removes thermodynamic support and causes initial collapse followed by runaway fusion, resulting in a pair-instability supernova.

- A sufficiently large and hot [stellar core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_core) may generate gamma-rays energetic enough to initiate photodisintegration directly, which will cause a complete collapse of the core.

The table below lists the known reasons for core collapse in massive stars, the types of stars in which they occur, their associated supernova type, and the remnant produced. The [metallicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallicity) is the proportion of elements other than hydrogen or helium, as compared to the Sun. The initial mass is the mass of the star prior to the supernova event, given in multiples of the Sun's mass, although the mass at the time of the supernova may be much lower.[^cite_note-heger-110]

| Cause of collapse | Progenitor star approximate initial mass ([solar masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass)) | Supernova type | Remnant |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Electron capture in a degenerate O+Ne+Mg core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_capture_supernova) | 9–10 | Faint II-P | Neutron star |
| Iron core collapse | 10–25 | Faint II-P | Neutron star |
| 25–40 with low or solar metallicity | Normal II-P | Black hole after fallback of material onto an initial neutron star |  |
| 25–40 with very high metallicity | II-L or II-b | Neutron star |  |
| 40–90 with low metallicity | None | Black hole |  |
| ≥ 40 with near-solar metallicity | Faint Ib/c, or [hypernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypernova) with [gamma-ray burst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma-ray_burst) (GRB) | Black hole after fallback of material onto an initial neutron star |  |
| ≥ 40 with very high metallicity | Ib/c | Neutron star |  |
| ≥ 90 with low metallicity | None, possible GRB | Black hole |  |
| Pair instability | 140–250 with low metallicity | II-P, sometimes a hypernova, possible GRB | No remnant |
| Photodisintegration | ≥ 250 with low metallicity | None (or luminous supernova?), possible GRB | Massive black hole |

Type IIn supernovae are not listed in the table. They can be produced by various types of core collapse in different progenitor stars, possibly even by Type Ia white dwarf ignitions, although it seems that most will be from iron core collapse in luminous [supergiants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergiant) or [hypergiants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypergiant) (including LBVs). The narrow spectral lines for which they are named occur because the supernova is expanding into a small dense cloud of circumstellar material.[^cite_note-moriya-113] It appears that a significant proportion of supposed Type IIn supernovae are supernova impostors, massive eruptions of LBV-like stars similar to the Great Eruption of [Eta Carinae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eta_Carinae). In these events, material previously ejected from the star creates the narrow absorption lines and causes a shock wave through interaction with the newly ejected material.[^cite_note-smith-114]

#### Detailed process



Within a massive, evolved star (a) the onion-layered shells of elements undergo fusion, forming an iron core (b) that reaches Chandrasekhar-mass and starts to collapse. The inner part of the core is compressed into neutrons (c), causing infalling material to bounce (d) and form an outward-propagating shock front (red). The shock starts to stall (e), but it is re-invigorated, likely by [neutrino heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_neutrinos). The surrounding material is blasted away (f), leaving only a degenerate remnant.

[^cite_note-Janka-2007-115]

When a stellar core is no longer supported against gravity, it collapses in on itself with velocities reaching 70,000 km/s (0.23[*c*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_of_light)),[^cite_note-grav_waves-116] resulting in a rapid increase in temperature and density. What follows depends on the mass and structure of the collapsing core, with low-mass degenerate cores forming neutron stars, higher-mass degenerate cores mostly collapsing completely to black holes, and non-degenerate cores undergoing runaway fusion.[^cite_note-Janka-2007-115][^cite_note-Hurley-2000-117]

The initial collapse of degenerate cores is accelerated by [beta decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_decay), photodisintegration and electron capture, which causes a burst of [electron neutrinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron_neutrino). As the density increases, [neutrino emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_neutrinos) is cut off as they become trapped in the core. The inner core eventually reaches typically 30 [km](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilometre) in diameter[^cite_note-WoosleyJanka-118] with a density comparable to that of an [atomic nucleus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_nucleus), and neutron [degeneracy pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degeneracy_pressure) tries to halt the collapse. If the core mass is more than about 15 solar masses then neutron degeneracy is insufficient to stop the collapse and a black hole forms directly with no supernova.[^cite_note-renzon2018-111]

In lower mass cores the collapse is stopped and the newly formed neutron core has an initial temperature of about 100 billion [Ks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin), 6,000 times the temperature of the [Sun's core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun's_core).[^cite_note-Janka-2007-115] At this temperature, neutrino-antineutrino pairs of all [flavours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino_oscillation) are efficiently formed by [thermal emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermal_radiation). These thermal neutrinos are several times more abundant than the electron-capture neutrinos.[^cite_note-119] About 10<sup>46</sup> joules, approximately 10% of the star's rest mass, is converted into a ten-second burst of neutrinos, which is the main output of the event.[^cite_note-WoosleyJanka-118][^cite_note-barwick-120] The suddenly halted core collapse rebounds and produces a shock wave that stalls in the outer core within milliseconds[^cite_note-121] as energy is lost through the dissociation of heavy elements. A process that is not clearly understood is necessary to allow the outer layers of the core to reabsorb around 10<sup>44</sup> joules[^cite_note-barwick-120] (1 [foe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foe_\(unit\))) from the [neutrino pulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_neutrinos), producing the visible brightness, although there are other theories that could power the explosion.[^cite_note-WoosleyJanka-118]

Some material from the outer envelope falls back onto the neutron star, and, for cores beyond about 8 M<sub>☉</sub>, there is sufficient fallback to form a black hole. This fallback will reduce the kinetic energy created and the mass of expelled radioactive material, but in some situations, it may also generate [relativistic jets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_jet) that result in a [gamma-ray burst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma-ray_burst) or an exceptionally luminous supernova.[^cite_note-piram2019-122]

The collapse of a massive non-degenerate core will ignite further fusion.[^cite_note-Hurley-2000-117] When the core collapse is initiated by [pair instability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pair-instability_supernova) ([photons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photon) turning into [electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron)-[positron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positron) pairs, thereby reducing the [radiation pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiation_pressure)) oxygen fusion begins and the collapse may be halted. For core masses of 40–60 M<sub>☉</sub>, the collapse halts and the star remains intact, but collapse will occur again when a larger core has formed. For cores of around 60–130 M<sub>☉</sub>, the fusion of oxygen and heavier elements is so energetic that the entire star is disrupted, causing a supernova. At the upper end of the mass range, the supernova is unusually luminous and extremely long-lived due to many solar masses of ejected <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni. For even larger core masses, the core temperature becomes high enough to allow photodisintegration and the core collapses completely into a black hole.[^cite_note-kasen-123][^cite_note-renzon2018-111]

#### Type II

Main article: [Type II supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_supernova)



The atypical subluminous Type II [SN 1997D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1997D)

Stars with initial masses less than about 8 M<sub>☉</sub> never develop a core large enough to collapse and they eventually lose their atmospheres to become white dwarfs. Stars with at least 9 M<sub>☉</sub> (possibly as much as 12 M<sub>☉</sub>[^cite_note-superagb-124]) evolve in a complex fashion, progressively burning heavier elements at hotter temperatures in their cores.[^cite_note-WoosleyJanka-118][^cite_note-science304-125] The star becomes layered like an onion, with the burning of more easily fused elements occurring in larger shells.[^cite_note-heger-110][^cite_note-hinshaw-126] Although popularly described as an onion with an iron core, the least massive supernova progenitors only have oxygen-[neon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neon)(-[magnesium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnesium)) cores. These [super-AGB stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super-AGB_star) may form the majority of core collapse supernovae, although less luminous and so less commonly observed than those from more massive progenitors.[^cite_note-superagb-124]

If core collapse occurs during a supergiant phase when the star still has a hydrogen envelope, the result is a Type II supernova.[^cite_note-Horiuchi_et_al_2014-127] The rate of mass loss for luminous stars depends on the metallicity and [luminosity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminosity). Extremely luminous stars at near solar metallicity will lose all their hydrogen before they reach core collapse and so will not form a supernova of Type II.[^cite_note-Horiuchi_et_al_2014-127] At low metallicity, all stars will reach core collapse with a hydrogen envelope but sufficiently massive stars collapse directly to a black hole without producing a visible supernova.[^cite_note-heger-110]

Stars with an initial mass up to about 90 times the Sun, or a little less at high metallicity, result in a Type II-P supernova, which is the most commonly observed type. At moderate to high metallicity, stars near the upper end of that mass range will have lost most of their hydrogen when core collapse occurs and the result will be a Type II-L supernova.[^cite_note-128] At very low metallicity, stars of around 140–250 M<sub>☉</sub> will reach core collapse by pair instability while they still have a hydrogen atmosphere and an oxygen core and the result will be a supernova with Type II characteristics but a very large mass of ejected <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni and high luminosity.[^cite_note-heger-110][^cite_note-129]

#### Type Ib and Ic

Main article: [Type Ib and Ic supernovae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_Ib_and_Ic_supernovae)



Type Ib SN 2008D

[^cite_note-130]

&#x20;at the far upper end of the galaxy, shown in [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray) (left) and visible light (right),

[^cite_note-131]

&#x20;with the brighter SN 2007uy closer to the centre

These supernovae, like those of Type II, are massive stars that undergo core collapse. Unlike the progenitors of Type II supernovae, the stars which become Type Ib and Type Ic supernovae have lost most of their outer (hydrogen) envelopes due to strong [stellar winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_wind) or else from interaction with a companion.[^cite_note-132] These stars are known as [Wolf–Rayet stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf%E2%80%93Rayet_stars), and they occur at moderate to high metallicity where continuum driven winds cause sufficiently high mass-loss rates. Observations of Type Ib/c supernova do not match the observed or expected occurrence of Wolf–Rayet stars. Alternate explanations for this type of core collapse supernova involve stars stripped of their hydrogen by binary interactions. Binary models provide a better match for the observed supernovae, with the proviso that no suitable binary helium stars have ever been observed.[^cite_note-eldridge-133]

Type Ib supernovae are more common than Type Ic and they result from Wolf–Rayet stars of [type WC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf-Rayet_star#Classification) which still have helium in their atmospheres. For a narrow range of masses, stars evolve further before reaching core collapse to become WO stars with very little helium remaining, and these are the progenitors of Type Ic supernovae.[^cite_note-134]

A few percent of the Type Ic supernovae are associated with [gamma-ray bursts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma-ray_burst) (GRB), though it is also believed that any hydrogen-stripped Type Ib or Ic supernova could produce a GRB, depending on the circumstances of the geometry.[^cite_note-135] The mechanism for producing this type of GRB is the jets produced by the magnetic field of the rapidly spinning [magnetar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetar) formed at the collapsing core of the star. The jets would also transfer energy into the expanding outer shell, producing a [super-luminous supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super-luminous_supernova).[^cite_note-piram2019-122][^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

Ultra-stripped supernovae occur when the exploding star has been stripped (almost) all the way to the metal core, via mass transfer in a close binary.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-TaurisLangerPodsiadlowski2015-139] As a result, very little material is ejected from the exploding star (c. 0.1 M<sub>☉</sub>). In the most extreme cases, ultra-stripped supernovae can occur in naked metal cores, barely above the Chandrasekhar mass limit. SN 2005ek[^cite_note-140] might be the first observational example of an ultra-stripped supernova, giving rise to a relatively dim and fast decaying light curve. The nature of ultra-stripped supernovae can be both iron core-collapse and electron capture supernovae, depending on the mass of the collapsing core. Ultra-stripped supernovae are believed to be associated with the second supernova explosion in a binary system, producing for example a tight double neutron star system.[^cite_note-TaurisKramerFreire2017-141][^cite_note-DeKasliwalOfek2018-142]

In 2022 a team of astronomers led by researchers from the [Weizmann Institute of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weizmann_Institute_of_Science) reported the first supernova explosion showing direct evidence for a Wolf-Rayet progenitor star. [SN 2019hgp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2019hgp?action=edit\&redlink=1) was a Type Icn supernova and is also the first in which the element neon has been detected.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]

#### &#x20;Electron-capture supernovae

In 1980, a "third type" of supernova was predicted by [Ken'ichi Nomoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken'ichi_Nomoto) of the [University of Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Tokyo), called an electron-capture supernova. It would arise when a star "in the transitional range (\~8 to 10 solar masses) between white dwarf formation and iron core-collapse supernovae", and with a [degenerate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degenerate_matter) O+Ne+Mg core,[^cite_note-HowellEtAl2021-01a-145] imploded after its core ran out of nuclear fuel, causing gravity to compress the electrons in the star's core into their atomic nuclei,[^cite_note-KeckObsNews2021-06-28-01a-146][^cite_note-RTE2021-06-28-01a-147] leading to a supernova explosion and leaving behind a neutron star.[^cite_note-heger-110] In June 2021, a paper in the journal *[Nature Astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_Astronomy)* reported that the 2018 supernova [SN 2018zd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2018zd) (in the galaxy [NGC 2146](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_2146), about 31 million light-years from [Earth](9228.md)) appeared to be the first observation of an electron-capture supernova.[^cite_note-HowellEtAl2021-01a-145][^cite_note-KeckObsNews2021-06-28-01a-146][^cite_note-RTE2021-06-28-01a-147] The 1054 supernova explosion that created the Crab Nebula in our galaxy had been thought to be the best candidate for an electron-capture supernova, and the 2021 paper makes it more likely that this was correct.[^cite_note-KeckObsNews2021-06-28-01a-146][^cite_note-RTE2021-06-28-01a-147]

### Failed supernovae

Main article: [Failed supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_supernova)

The core collapse of some massive stars may not result in a visible supernova. This happens if the initial core collapse cannot be reversed by the mechanism that produces an explosion, usually because the core is too massive. These events are difficult to detect, but large surveys have detected possible candidates.[^cite_note-failed-148][^cite_note-gerke-149] The [red supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_supergiant) [N6946-BH1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N6946-BH1) in [NGC 6946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGC_6946) underwent a modest outburst in March 2009, before fading from view. Only a faint [infrared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrared) source remains at the star's location.[^cite_note-adams-150]

### Light curves



Typical light curves for several types of supernovae; in practice, magnitude and duration varies within each type. See Karttunen et al. for Type Ia, Ib, II-L and II-P;

[^cite_note-Springer-2016-151]

&#x20;Modjaz et al. for Type Ic and IIb;

[^cite_note-Modjaz-2019-152]

&#x20;and Nyholm et al. for Type IIn.

[^cite_note-Nyholm-2020-153]

The material ejected by a supernova would dim quickly without some energy input to keep them hot. The source of this energy—which can maintain the optical emission for months—was, at first, a puzzle. Some considered [rotational energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotational_energy) from the central pulsar as a source.[^cite_note-154] Although the energy that initially powers each type of supernovae is delivered promptly, the light curves are dominated by subsequent radioactive heating of the rapidly expanding ejecta. The intensely radioactive nature of the ejecta gases was first calculated on sound nucleosynthesis grounds in the late 1960s, and this has since been demonstrated as correct for most supernovae.[^cite_note-155] It was not until [SN 1987A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_1987A) that direct observation of gamma-ray lines unambiguously identified the major radioactive nuclei.[^cite_note-S.M._Matz,_G.H._Share_1988-156]

It is now known by direct observation that much of the light curve (the graph of luminosity as a function of time) after the occurrence of a [type II supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_II_Supernova), such as SN 1987A, is explained by those predicted radioactive decays.[^cite_note-\:0-15] Although the luminous emission consists of optical photons, it is the radioactive power absorbed by the ejected gases that keeps the remnant hot enough to radiate light. The radioactive decay of <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni through its daughters <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co to <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Fe produces gamma-ray [photons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photon), primarily with energies of 847 [keV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronvolt) and 1,238 keV, that are absorbed and dominate the heating and thus the luminosity of the ejecta at intermediate times (several weeks) to late times (several months).[^cite_note-157] Energy for the peak of the light curve of SN1987A was provided by the decay of [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_nickel) to <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co (half-life 6 days) while energy for the later light curve in particular fit very closely with the 77.3-day half-life of [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isotopes_of_cobalt) decaying to <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Fe. Later measurements by space gamma-ray telescopes of the small fraction of the <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co and <sup>57</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co gamma rays that escaped the SN 1987A remnant without absorption confirmed earlier predictions that those two radioactive nuclei were the power sources.[^cite_note-S.M._Matz,_G.H._Share_1988-156]



[Messier 61](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messier_61) with supernova SN2020jfo, taken by an amateur astronomer in 2020

The late-time decay phase of visual light curves for different supernova types all depend on radioactive heating, but they vary in shape and amplitude because of the underlying mechanisms, the way that visible radiation is produced, the epoch of its observation, and the transparency of the ejected material.[^cite_note-158] The light curves can be significantly different at other wavelengths. For example, at ultraviolet wavelengths there is an early extremely luminous peak lasting only a few hours corresponding to the breakout of the shock launched by the initial event, but that breakout is hardly detectable optically.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]

The light curves for Type Ia are mostly very uniform, with a consistent maximum absolute magnitude and a relatively steep decline in luminosity. Their optical energy output is driven by radioactive decay of ejected <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni (half-life 6 days), which then decays to radioactive <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co (half-life 77 days). These radioisotopes excite the surrounding material to incandescence.[^cite_note-explosion_model-92] Modern studies of cosmology rely on <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni radioactivity providing the energy for the optical brightness of supernovae of Type Ia, which are the "standard candles" of cosmology but whose diagnostic 847 keV and 1,238 keV gamma rays were first detected only in 2014.[^cite_note-161] The initial phases of the light curve decline steeply as the effective size of the [photosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photosphere) decreases and trapped electromagnetic radiation is depleted. The light curve continues to decline in the [B band](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UBV_photometric_system) while it may show a small shoulder in the visual at about 40 days, but this is only a hint of a secondary maximum that occurs in the infra-red as certain ionised heavy elements recombine to produce infra-red radiation and the ejecta become transparent to it. The visual light curve continues to decline at a rate slightly greater than the decay rate of the radioactive cobalt (which has the longer half-life and controls the later curve), because the ejected material becomes more diffuse and less able to convert the high energy radiation into visual radiation. After several months, the light curve changes its decline rate again as [positron emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positron_emission) from the remaining <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co becomes dominant, although this portion of the light curve has been little-studied.[^cite_note-taubenberger2009-162]

Type Ib and Ic light curves are similar to type Ia although with a lower average peak luminosity. The visual light output is again due to radioactive decay being converted into visual radiation, but there is a much lower mass of the created <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni. The peak luminosity varies considerably and there are even occasional Type Ib/c supernovae orders of magnitude more and less luminous than the norm. The most luminous Type Ic supernovae are referred to as [hypernovae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypernova) and tend to have broadened light curves in addition to the increased peak luminosity. The source of the extra energy is thought to be relativistic jets driven by the formation of a rotating black hole, which also produce gamma-ray bursts.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-arxiv-164]

The light curves for type II supernovae are characterised by a much slower decline than Type I, on the order of 0.05 magnitudes per day,[^cite_note-barbon1979-77] excluding the plateau phase. The visual light output is dominated by kinetic energy rather than radioactive decay for several months, due primarily to the existence of hydrogen in the ejecta from the atmosphere of the supergiant progenitor star. In the initial destruction this hydrogen becomes heated and ionised. The majority of Type II supernovae show a prolonged plateau in their light curves as this hydrogen recombines, emitting visible light and becoming more transparent. This is then followed by a declining light curve driven by radioactive decay although slower than in type I supernovae, due to the efficiency of conversion into light by all the hydrogen.[^cite_note-doggett-69]

In type II-L the plateau is absent because the progenitor had relatively little hydrogen left in its atmosphere, sufficient to appear in the spectrum but insufficient to produce a noticeable plateau in the light output. In type IIb supernovae the hydrogen atmosphere of the progenitor is so depleted (thought to be due to tidal stripping by a companion star) that the light curve is closer to a Type I supernova and the hydrogen even disappears from the spectrum after several weeks.[^cite_note-doggett-69]

Type IIn supernovae are characterised by additional narrow spectral lines produced in a dense shell of circumstellar material. Their light curves are generally very broad and extended, occasionally also extremely luminous and referred to as a superluminous supernova. These light curves are produced by the highly efficient conversion of kinetic energy of the ejecta into electromagnetic radiation by interaction with the dense shell of material. This only occurs when the material is sufficiently dense and compact, indicating that it has been produced by the progenitor star itself only shortly before the supernova occurs.[^cite_note-fillipenko1997-165][^cite_note-pastorello2002-166]

Large numbers of supernovae have been catalogued and classified to provide [distance candles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_candle) and test models.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Average characteristics vary somewhat with distance and type of host galaxy, but can broadly be specified for each supernova type.

| Type<sup>[a](#endnote_a)</sup> | Average peak [absolute magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_magnitude)<sup>[b](#endnote_b)</sup> | Approximate energy ([foe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foe_\(unit\)))<sup>[c](#endnote_c)</sup> | Days to peak luminosity | Days from peak to 10% luminosity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ia | −19 | 1 | approx. 19 | around 60 |
| Ib/c (faint) | around −15 | 0.1 | 15–25 | unknown |
| Ib | around −17 | 1 | 15–25 | 40–100 |
| Ic | around −16 | 1 | 15–25 | 40–100 |
| Ic (bright) | to −22 | above 5 | roughly 25 | roughly 100 |
| II-b | around −17 | 1 | around 20 | around 100 |
| II-L | around −17 | 1 | around 13 | around 150 |
| II-P (faint) | around −14 | 0.1 | roughly 15 | unknown |
| II-P | around −16 | 1 | around 15 | Plateau then around 50 |
| IIn<sup>[d](#endnote_d)</sup> | around −17 | 1 | 12–30 or more | 50–150 |
| IIn (bright) | to −22 | above 5 | above 50 | above 100 |

Notes:

1. **[^](#ref_a)**&#x46;aint types may be a distinct sub-class. Bright types may be a continuum from slightly over-luminous to hypernovae.

2. **[^](#ref_b)**&#x54;hese magnitudes are measured in the R band. Measurements in V or B bands are common and will be around half a magnitude brighter for supernovae.

3. **[^](#ref_c)**[Order of magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_magnitude) kinetic energy. Total electromagnetic radiated energy is usually lower, (theoretical) neutrino energy much higher.

4. **[^](#ref_d)**&#x50;robably a heterogeneous group, any of the other types embedded in nebulosity.

### Asymmetry



The [pulsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulsar) in the [Crab Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crab_Nebula) is travelling at 375 km/s relative to the nebula.

[^cite_note-171]

A long-standing puzzle surrounding type II supernovae is why the remaining compact object receives a large velocity away from the centre of mass;[^cite_note-Höflich-2004-172] [pulsars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulsar), and thus neutron stars, are observed to have high [peculiar velocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peculiar_velocity), and black holes presumably do as well, although they are far harder to observe in isolation. The initial impetus can be substantial, propelling an object of more than a solar mass at a velocity of 500 km/s or greater. This indicates an expansion asymmetry, but the mechanism by which momentum is transferred to the compact object remains a puzzle. Proposed explanations for this kick include convection in the collapsing star, asymmetric ejection of matter during [neutron star formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star_formation), and asymmetrical [neutrino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino) emissions.[^cite_note-Höflich-2004-172][^cite_note-Janka-2022-173]

One possible explanation for this asymmetry is large-scale [convection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convection) above the core. The convection can create radial variations in density giving rise to variations in the amount of energy absorbed from neutrino outflow.[^cite_note-Janka-2007-115] However analysis of this mechanism predicts only modest momentum transfer.[^cite_note-Fryer-2004-174] Another possible explanation is that accretion of gas onto the central neutron star can create a [disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_disk) that drives highly directional jets, propelling matter at a high velocity out of the star, and driving transverse shocks that completely disrupt the star. These jets might play a crucial role in the resulting supernova.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] (A similar model is used for explaining long gamma-ray bursts.) The dominant mechanism may depend upon the mass of the progenitor star.[^cite_note-Janka-2022-173]

Initial asymmetries have also been confirmed in type Ia supernovae through observation. This result may mean that the initial luminosity of this type of supernova depends on the viewing angle. However, the expansion becomes more symmetrical with the passage of time. Early asymmetries are detectable by measuring the polarisation of the emitted light.[^cite_note-177]

### Energy output



The radioactive decays of <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni and <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co that produce a supernova visible light curve

[^cite_note-explosion_model-92]

[^cite_note-mazzali-178]

Although supernovae are primarily known as luminous events, the [electromagnetic radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_radiation) they release is almost a minor side-effect. Particularly in the case of core collapse supernovae, the emitted electromagnetic radiation is a tiny fraction of the total energy released during the event.[^cite_note-179]

There is a fundamental difference between the balance of energy production in the different types of supernova. In Type Ia white dwarf detonations, most of the energy is directed into [heavy element synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosynthesis) and the [kinetic energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinetic_energy) of the ejecta.[^cite_note-180] In core collapse supernovae, the vast majority of the energy is directed into [neutrino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_neutrino) emission, and while some of this apparently powers the observed destruction, 99%+ of the neutrinos escape the star in the first few minutes following the start of the collapse.[^cite_note-Antonioli-2004-51]

Standard Type Ia supernovae derive their energy from a runaway nuclear fusion of a carbon-oxygen white dwarf. The details of the energetics are still not fully understood, but the result is the ejection of the entire mass of the original star at high kinetic energy. Around half a solar mass of that mass is [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni-56) generated from [silicon burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicon-burning_process). <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni is [radioactive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive) and decays into [<sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-56) by [beta plus decay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_plus_decay) (with a [half life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half_life) of six days) and gamma rays. <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Co itself decays by the beta plus ([positron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positron)) path with a half-life of 77 days into stable <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Fe. These two processes are responsible for the electromagnetic radiation from Type Ia supernovae. In combination with the changing transparency of the ejected material, they produce the rapidly declining light curve.[^cite_note-mazzali-178]

Core collapse supernovae are on average visually fainter than Type Ia supernovae,[^cite_note-Springer-2016-151][^cite_note-Modjaz-2019-152][^cite_note-Nyholm-2020-153] but the total energy released is far higher, as outlined in the following table.

| Supernova | Approximate total energy x10<sup>44</sup> joules ([foe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foe_\(unit\)))<sup>[c](#endnote_c)</sup> | Ejected Ni (solar masses) | Neutrino energy (foe) | Kinetic energy (foe) | Electromagnetic radiation (foe) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Type Ia[^cite_note-mazzali-178][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] | 1.5 | 0.4 – 0.8 | 0.1 | 1.3 – 1.4 | \~0.01 |
| Core collapse[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184] | 100 | (0.01) – 1 | 100 | 1 | 0.001 – 0.01 |
| Hypernova | 100 | \~1 | 1–100 | 1–100 | \~0.1 |
| Pair instability[^cite_note-kasen-123] | 5–100 | 0.5 – 50 | low? | 1–100 | 0.01 – 0.1 |

In some core collapse supernovae, fallback onto a black hole drives [relativistic jets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_jet) which may produce a brief energetic and directional burst of gamma rays and also transfers substantial further energy into the ejected material. This is one scenario for producing high-luminosity supernovae and is thought to be the cause of Type Ic hypernovae and long-duration gamma-ray bursts.[^cite_note-185] If the relativistic jets are too brief and fail to penetrate the stellar envelope then a low-luminosity gamma-ray burst may be produced and the supernova may be sub-luminous.[^cite_note-186]

When a supernova occurs inside a small dense cloud of circumstellar material, it will produce a shock wave that can efficiently convert a high fraction of the kinetic energy into electromagnetic radiation. Even though the initial energy was entirely normal the resulting supernova will have high luminosity and extended duration since it does not rely on exponential radioactive decay. This type of event may cause Type IIn hypernovae.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188]

Although pair-instability supernovae are core collapse supernovae with spectra and light curves similar to Type II-P, the nature after core collapse is more like that of a giant Type Ia with runaway fusion of carbon, oxygen and silicon. The total energy released by the highest-mass events is comparable to other core collapse supernovae but neutrino production is thought to be very low, hence the kinetic and electromagnetic energy released is very high. The cores of these stars are much larger than any white dwarf and the amount of radioactive nickel and other heavy elements ejected from their cores can be orders of magnitude higher, with consequently high visual luminosity.[^cite_note-189]

### Progenitor

Occasional supernovae appear in this sped-up artist's impression of distant galaxies. Each exploding star briefly rivals the brightness of its host galaxy.

The supernova classification type is closely tied to the type of progenitor star at the time of the collapse. The occurrence of each type of supernova depends on the star's metallicity, since this affects the strength of the stellar wind and thereby the rate at which the star loses mass.[^cite_note-190]

Type Ia supernovae are produced from white dwarf stars in binary star systems and occur in all [galaxy types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_morphological_classification).[^cite_note-191] Core collapse supernovae are only found in galaxies undergoing current or very recent star formation, since they result from short-lived massive stars. They are most commonly found in type Sc spirals, but also in the arms of other spiral galaxies and in [irregular galaxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irregular_galaxy), especially [starburst galaxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starburst_galaxy).[^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194]

Type Ib and Ic supernovae are hypothesised to have been produced by core collapse of massive stars that have lost their outer layer of hydrogen and helium, either via strong stellar winds or mass transfer to a companion.[^cite_note-arxiv-164] They normally occur in regions of new star formation, and are extremely rare in [elliptical galaxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elliptical_galaxy).[^cite_note-Perets-2010-74] The progenitors of Type IIn supernovae also have high rates of mass loss in the period just prior to their explosions.[^cite_note-195] Type Ic supernovae have been observed to occur in regions that are more metal-rich and have higher star-formation rates than average for their host galaxies.[^cite_note-196] The table shows the progenitor for the main types of core collapse supernova, and the approximate proportions that have been observed in the local neighbourhood.

| Type | Progenitor star | Fraction |
| --- | --- | --- |
