| Ib | WC [Wolf–Rayet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf%E2%80%93Rayet_star) or [helium star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helium_star) | 9.0% |
| Ic | WO [Wolf–Rayet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf%E2%80%93Rayet_star) | 17.0% |
| II-P | [Supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergiant) | 55.5% |
| II-L | [Supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergiant) with a depleted hydrogen shell | 3.0% |
| IIn | [Supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergiant) in a dense cloud of expelled material (such as [LBV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminous_blue_variable)) | 2.4% |
| IIb | [Supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supergiant) with highly depleted hydrogen (stripped by companion?) | 12.1% |
| IIpec | [Blue supergiant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_supergiant) | 1.0% |



Supernova types by initial mass-metallicity



Remnants of single massive stars

There are a number of difficulties reconciling modelled and observed stellar evolution leading up to core collapse supernovae. Red supergiants are the progenitors for the vast majority of core collapse supernovae, and these have been observed but only at relatively low masses and luminosities, below about 18 M<sub>☉</sub> and 100,000 L<sub>☉</sub>, respectively. Most progenitors of Type II supernovae are not detected and must be considerably fainter, and presumably less massive. This discrepancy has been referred to as the **red supergiant problem**.[^cite_note-davies2020-197] It was first described in 2009 by Stephen Smartt, who also coined the term. After performing a volume-limited search for supernovae, Smartt et al. found the lower and upper mass limits for Type II-P supernovae to form to be 8.5+1\
−1.5 M<sub>☉</sub> and 16.5±1.5 M<sub>☉</sub>, respectively. The former is consistent with the expected upper mass limits for white dwarf progenitors to form, but the latter is not consistent with massive star populations in the Local Group.[^cite_note-smartt2009-198] The upper limit for red supergiants that produce a visible supernova explosion has been calculated at 19+4\
−2 *M*<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-davies2020-197]

It is thought that higher mass red supergiants do not explode as supernovae, but instead evolve back towards hotter temperatures. Several progenitors of Type IIb supernovae have been confirmed, and these were K and G supergiants, plus one A supergiant.[^cite_note-smartt-199] Yellow hypergiants or LBVs are proposed progenitors for Type IIb supernovae, and almost all Type IIb supernovae near enough to observe have shown such progenitors.[^cite_note-problem-200][^cite_note-ysg-201]

Infographic showing arrows between circles representing stellar evolution and how it varies by mass

Approximate stellar evolution pathways of supernova progenitor stars (and lower mass stars) with circle size reflecting relative size and color related to temperature

Blue supergiants form an unexpectedly high proportion of confirmed supernova progenitors, partly due to their high luminosity and easy detection, while not a single Wolf–Rayet progenitor has yet been clearly identified.[^cite_note-smartt-199][^cite_note-yoon-202] Models have had difficulty showing how blue supergiants lose enough mass to reach supernova without progressing to a different evolutionary stage. One study has shown a possible route for low-luminosity post-red supergiant luminous blue variables to collapse, most likely as a Type IIn supernova.[^cite_note-lbv-203] Several examples of hot luminous progenitors of Type IIn supernovae have been detected: [SN 2005gy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2005gy?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [SN 2010jl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2010jl) were both apparently massive luminous stars, but are very distant; and [SN 2009ip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SN_2009ip) had a highly luminous progenitor likely to have been an LBV, but is a peculiar supernova whose exact nature is disputed.[^cite_note-smartt-199]

The progenitors of Type Ib/c supernovae are not observed at all, and constraints on their possible luminosity are often lower than those of known [WC stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WC_star).[^cite_note-smartt-199] [WO stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WO_star) are extremely rare and visually relatively faint, so it is difficult to say whether such progenitors are missing or just yet to be observed. Very luminous progenitors have not been securely identified, despite numerous supernovae being observed near enough that such progenitors would have been clearly imaged.[^cite_note-yoon-202] Population modelling shows that the observed Type Ib/c supernovae could be reproduced by a mixture of single massive stars and stripped-envelope stars from interacting binary systems.[^cite_note-eldridge-133] The continued lack of unambiguous detection of progenitors for normal Type Ib and Ic supernovae may be due to most massive stars collapsing directly to a black hole [without a supernova outburst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_supernova). Most of these supernovae are then produced from lower-mass low-luminosity helium stars in binary systems. A small number would be from rapidly rotating massive stars, likely corresponding to the highly energetic Type Ic-BL events that are associated with long-duration gamma-ray bursts.[^cite_note-smartt-199]

## External impact

Supernovae events generate heavier elements that are scattered throughout the surrounding interstellar medium. The expanding shock wave from a supernova can trigger star formation. Galactic cosmic rays are generated by supernova explosions.[^cite_note-204]

### Source of heavy elements

Main articles: [Stellar nucleosynthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_nucleosynthesis) and [Supernova nucleosynthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_nucleosynthesis)



Periodic table showing the source of each element in the interstellar medium

Supernovae are a major source of elements in the interstellar medium from oxygen through to rubidium,[^cite_note-nucleosynthesis-205][^cite_note-François-206][^cite_note-Truran-207] though the theoretical abundances of the elements produced or seen in the spectra varies significantly depending on the various supernova types.[^cite_note-Truran-207] Type Ia supernovae produce mainly silicon and iron-peak elements, metals such as nickel and iron.[^cite_note-nomoto2018-208][^cite_note-maeda-209] Core collapse supernovae eject much smaller quantities of the iron-peak elements than Type Ia supernovae, but larger masses of light [alpha elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_element) such as oxygen and neon, and elements heavier than zinc. The latter is especially true with electron capture supernovae.[^cite_note-210] The bulk of the material ejected by Type II supernovae is hydrogen and helium.[^cite_note-eichler-211] The heavy elements are produced by: nuclear fusion for nuclei up to <sup>34</sup>\
<sub></sub>S; silicon photodisintegration rearrangement and quasiequilibrium during silicon burning for nuclei between <sup>36</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ar and <sup>56</sup>\
<sub></sub>Ni; and rapid capture of neutrons ([r-process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R-process)) during the supernova's collapse for elements heavier than iron.  The r-process produces highly unstable nuclei that are rich in [neutrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron) and that rapidly beta decay into more stable forms. In supernovae, r-process reactions are responsible for about half of all the isotopes of elements beyond iron,[^cite_note-212] although [neutron star mergers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star_merger) may be the main astrophysical source for many of these elements.[^cite_note-nucleosynthesis-205][^cite_note-siegel-213]

In the modern universe, old [asymptotic giant branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asymptotic_giant_branch) (AGB) stars are the dominant source of dust from oxides, carbon and [s-process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S-process) elements.[^cite_note-nucleosynthesis-205][^cite_note-214] However, in the early universe, before AGB stars formed, supernovae may have been the main source of dust.[^cite_note-dust-215]

### Role in stellar evolution

Main article: [Supernova remnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernova_remnant)

Remnants of many supernovae consist of a compact object and a rapidly expanding shock wave of material. This cloud of material sweeps up surrounding [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium) during a free expansion phase, which can last for up to two centuries. The wave then gradually undergoes a period of [adiabatic expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabatic_process), and will slowly cool and mix with the surrounding interstellar medium over a period of about 10,000 years.[^cite_note-216]



Supernova remnant N 63A lies within a clumpy region of gas and dust in the [Large Magellanic Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Magellanic_Cloud).

The [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang) produced hydrogen, [helium](13256.md) and traces of [lithium](17561.md), while all heavier elements are synthesised in stars, supernovae, and collisions between neutron stars (thus being indirectly due to supernovae). Supernovae tend to enrich the surrounding interstellar medium with elements other than hydrogen and helium, which usually astronomers refer to as "metals".[^cite_note-Johnson2019-217] These ejected elements ultimately enrich the [molecular clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_cloud) that are the sites of star formation.[^cite_note-218] Thus, each stellar generation has a slightly different composition, going from an almost pure mixture of hydrogen and helium to a more metal-rich composition. Supernovae are the dominant mechanism for distributing these heavier elements, which are formed in a star during its period of nuclear fusion. The different abundances of elements in the material that forms a star have important influences on the star's life,[^cite_note-Johnson2019-217][^cite_note-219] and may influence the possibility of having [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet) orbiting it: more [giant planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant_planet) form around stars of higher metallicity.[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]

The kinetic energy of an expanding supernova remnant can trigger star formation by compressing nearby, dense molecular clouds in space.[^cite_note-222] The increase in turbulent pressure can also prevent star formation if the cloud is unable to lose the excess energy.[^cite_note-aaa128-223]

Evidence from daughter products of short-lived [radioactive isotopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radioactive_isotope) shows that a nearby supernova helped determine the composition of the [Solar System](26903.md) 4.5 billion years ago, and may even have triggered the formation of this system.[^cite_note-224]

[Fast radio bursts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_radio_burst) (FRBs) are intense, transient pulses of radio waves that typically last no more than milliseconds. Many explanations for these events have been proposed; [magnetars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetar) produced by core-collapse supernovae are leading candidates.[^cite_note-AJL-20200601-225][^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228]

### Cosmic rays

Supernova remnants are thought to accelerate a large fraction of galactic primary [cosmic rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_ray), but direct evidence for cosmic ray production has only been found in a small number of remnants. Gamma rays from [pion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pion)-decay have been detected from the supernova remnants [IC 443](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IC_443) and W44. These are produced when accelerated [protons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proton) from the remnant impact on interstellar material.[^cite_note-ackermann-2013-229]

### Gravitational waves

Supernovae are thought to be sources of [gravitational waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_wave) detectable in our Galaxy,[^cite_note-230] but no supernovae have occurred in the Milky Way recently. The only gravitational wave events so far detected are from mergers of black holes and neutron stars, probable remnants of supernovae.[^cite_note-morozova-231] Like the neutrino emissions, the gravitational waves produced by a core-collapse supernova are expected to arrive before the light generated by the subsequent explosion. Consequently, they may provide information about the core-collapse process that is unavailable by other means. Most gravitational-wave signals predicted by supernova models are short in duration, lasting less than a second, and thus difficult to detect. Using the arrival of a neutrino signal may provide a trigger that can identify the time window in which to seek the gravitational wave emission, helping to distinguish the latter from background noise.[^cite_note-232]

### Effect on Earth

Main article: [Near-Earth supernova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-Earth_supernova)

A near-Earth supernova is a supernova close enough to the Earth to have noticeable effects on its [biosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosphere). Depending upon the type and energy of the supernova, it could be as far as 3,000 light-years away. In 1996 it was theorised that traces of past supernovae might be detectable on Earth in the form of metal isotope signatures in [rock strata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_strata). [Iron-60](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron#Isotopes) enrichment was later reported in deep-sea rock of the [Pacific Ocean](23070.md).[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234][^cite_note-Fields-235] In 2009, elevated levels of nitrate ions were found in Antarctic ice, which coincided with the 1006 and 1054 supernovae. Gamma rays from these supernovae could have boosted atmospheric levels of nitrogen oxides, which became trapped in the ice.[^cite_note-236]

Historically, nearby supernovae may have influenced the [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity) of life on the planet. Geological records suggest that nearby supernova events have led to an increase in cosmic rays, which in turn produced a cooler climate. A greater temperature difference between the poles and the equator created stronger winds, increased ocean mixing, and resulted in the transport of [nutrients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrients) to shallow waters along the [continental shelves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelves). This led to greater biodiversity.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238]

Type Ia supernovae are thought to be potentially the most dangerous if they occur close enough to the Earth. Because these supernovae arise from dim, common white dwarf stars in binary systems, it is likely that a supernova that can affect the Earth will occur unpredictably and in a star system that is not well studied. The closest-known candidate is [IK Pegasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IK_Pegasi) (HR 8210), about 150 light-years away,[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] but observations suggest that it could be as long as 1.9 billion years before the white dwarf can accrete the critical mass required to become a Type Ia supernova.[^cite_note-241]

According to a 2003 estimate, a Type II supernova would have to be closer than 8 [parsecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec) (26 light-years) to destroy half of the Earth's ozone layer, and there are no such candidates closer than about 500 light-years.[^cite_note-Gehrels-242]

## Milky Way candidates

Main article: [List of supernova candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_supernova_candidates)



The [nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf%E2%80%93Rayet_nebula) around [Wolf–Rayet star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf%E2%80%93Rayet_star) WR124, which is located at a distance of about 21,000 [light-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year)

[^cite_note-243]

The next supernova in the Milky Way will likely be detectable even if it occurs on the far side of the galaxy. It is likely to be produced by the collapse of an unremarkable red supergiant, and it is very probable that it will already have been catalogued in infrared surveys such as [2MASS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2MASS). There is a smaller chance that the next core collapse supernova will be produced by a different type of massive star such as a yellow hypergiant, luminous blue variable, or Wolf–Rayet. The chances of the next supernova being a Type Ia produced by a white dwarf are calculated to be about a third of those for a core collapse supernova. Again it should be observable wherever it occurs, but it is less likely that the progenitor will ever have been observed. It is not even known exactly what a Type Ia progenitor system looks like, and it is difficult to detect them beyond a few parsecs. The total supernova rate in the Milky Way is estimated to be between 2 and 12 per century, although one has not actually been observed for several centuries.[^cite_note-adams-150]

Statistically, the most common variety of core-collapse supernova is Type II-P, and the progenitors of this type are red supergiants.[^cite_note-244] It is difficult to identify which of those supergiants are in the final stages of heavy element fusion in their cores and which have millions of years left. The most-massive red supergiants shed their atmospheres and evolve to Wolf–Rayet stars before their cores collapse. All Wolf–Rayet stars end their lives from the Wolf–Rayet phase within a million years or so, but again it is difficult to identify those that are closest to core collapse. One class that is expected to have no more than a few thousand years before exploding are the WO Wolf–Rayet stars, which are known to have exhausted their core helium.[^cite_note-245] Only eight of them are known, and only four of those are in the Milky Way.[^cite_note-tramper-246]

A number of close or well-known stars have been identified as possible core collapse supernova candidates: the high-mass blue stars [Spica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spica), [Rigel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigel) and [Deneb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deneb),[^cite_note-247] the red supergiants [Betelgeuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Betelgeuse), [Antares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antares), and [VV Cephei A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VV_Cephei);[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249][^cite_note-\:2-250] the yellow hypergiant [Rho Cassiopeiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rho_Cassiopeiae);[^cite_note-251] the luminous blue variable Eta Carinae that has already produced a supernova impostor;[^cite_note-252] and both components, a blue supergiant and a Wolf–Rayet star, of the Regor or [Gamma Velorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_Velorum) system.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254] [Mimosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimosa_\(star\)), [Acrux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acrux) and Hadar or [Beta Centauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_Centauri), three bright star systems in the southern constellation of [Crux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crux) and [Centaurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaurus) respectively, each contain blue stars with sufficient masses to explode as supernovae.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257] Others have gained notoriety as possible, although not very likely, progenitors for a gamma-ray burst; for example [WR 104](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WR_104).[^cite_note-258]

Identification of candidates for a Type Ia supernova is much more speculative. Any binary with an accreting white dwarf might produce a supernova, although the exact mechanism and timescale is still debated. These systems are faint and difficult to identify, but the novae and [recurrent novae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recurrent_nova) are such systems that conveniently advertise themselves via outburst. One example is [U Scorpii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U_Scorpii).[^cite_note-259]



Some of the closest core-collapse supernova candidates to Earth within one kiloparsec, most of which are K-type red supergiants

[^cite_note-\:2-250]
